Properties of tensorial free cumulants
Thomas Buc–d’Alché, Luca Lionni

Abstract
In the past two years, several points of view have been proposed to address the question of the generalization of the theory of free probability to random tensors with different invariances, and it is unclear at this point whether they lead to the same notions of tensorial free cumulants and freeness. One way to approach this problem, developed by Collins, Gurau and the second named author for local unitary invariant random tensors, relies on finite size quantities involving averages over the invariance group, and whose asymptotics naturally possess the properties expected for tensorial generalizations of free cumulants of arbitrary orders. At this point, this approach has only been carried out for certain distributions, and for a subset of the moments that define such theories, and a more systematic and exhaustive study is lacking. This is the program initiated in this paper: we link this approach to the one proposed by Nechita and Park; extend a number of their results as well as those of the aforementioned paper to arbitrary orders of fluctuations, thereby generalizing higher order free cumulants; push further the study of distributions with larger invariance groups; detail the link with the asymptotics of the free-energies of the tensor HCIZ and BGW integrals; and provide formulae for tensorial free cumulants of products of tensors. Another important question is that of the definition of concrete distributions whose tensorial free-cumulants take non-trivial values. We compute the tensorial free cumulants for Gaussian random tensors with non-trivial covariances, and show that they provide such examples.



1
Introduction

Throughout this paper, tensors on
D
inputs are arrays or complex numbers of the form
T=(T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{D}})
or
A=(A_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{D};j_{1},\ldots,j_{D}})
, where the indices
i_{c},j_{c}
take value in
[N]=\{1,\ldots,N\}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
1
1

1

We sometimes consider more generally indices
i_{c},j_{c}
taking value in
[N_{c}]=\{1,\ldots,N_{c}\}
,
N_{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, but we then explicitly say so.
Adopting the vocabulary from quantum physics, tensors of the former kind are called
pure
, while those of the second kind are called
mixed
.
Recent advances in several domains are calling for a better mathematical understanding of
random
tensors. Applications include quantum information
[
Hay+16
,
AS17
,
CN15
,
DLN20
,
Che+24
,
PWW23
,
IN25
,
DM24
,
CCL26
]
, quantum gravity
[
Bon+11
,
Lio18
,
Gur17
,
Gur16
,
Hay+16
,
PWW23
,
IN25
]
, data science and statistics
[
MR14
,
BGP24
,
KMW24
]
, condensed matter (e.g. tensor networks)
[
Orú19
,
LP22
]
, and statistical physics (e.g. spin glasses)
[
CS92
,
CC05
,
DM24
]
, random hyper graphs and community detection
[
FW95
]
, etc.

Local unitary (LU) transformations result from the action of tensor products
U=U_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{D}
of
N\times N
unitary matrices
U_{c}\in U(N)
on pure and mixed tensors, respectively by left multiplication
T\rightarrow UT
and conjugation
A\rightarrow UAU^{\dagger}
, where:

\displaystyle(UT)_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{D}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b_{1},\ldots,b_{D}=1}^{N}(U_{1})_{i_{1},a_{1}}\cdots(U_{D})_{i_{D},a_{D}}T_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{D}}\;,

(1)

\displaystyle(UAU^{\dagger})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{D};j_{1},\ldots,j_{D}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{a_{c},b_{c}\in[N]\}_{1\leq c\leq D}}A_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{D};b_{1},\ldots,b_{D}}\prod_{c=1}^{D}(U_{c})_{i_{c},a_{c}}(\bar{U_{c}})_{j_{c},b_{c}}\;.

(2)

A random tensor is said to be
LU-invariant
if its distributions is invariant under LU transformations. The appropriate notion of (macroscopic) moments in this context are the expectations of a family of polynomials in the components of the tensors called trace-invariants, invariant under LU-transformations, which separate the LU orbits, generate the ring of LU invariant polynomials, and are asymptotically linearly independent (see
[
CGL25
,
CCL26
]
where these properties are stated and proven, and in which the a number of original references are given). These trace-invariants are labeled by
D
-tuples of permutations, up to relabeling equivalence. The data of these trace-invariants and of the LU-orbit generalizes the data of the spectrum and unitary orbit. A trace-invariant is connected if it does not decompose as a product of smaller trace-invariants (if the tuple that labels it satisfies some transitivity condition).

In quantum physics, a quantum system is modeled by a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, and pure and mixed quantum states respectively correspond to normalized elements
\lvert\varphi\rangle
of
\mathcal{H}
, and Hermitian, positive trace-one operators
\rho
on
\mathcal{H}
. Entanglement
[
Hor+09
,
WGE16
]

is a correlation between the different sub-spaces
\mathcal{H}_{c}
of a quantum system
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{D}
, important for its technological applications and implications in e.g. condensed matter theory and quantum gravity. Pure and mixed tensors are then seen respectively as the coefficients of the expansions of pure and mixed quantum states on the canonical product basis of
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{D}
. LU-invariance is important in this context: LU transformations do not affect entanglement, and the orbits under these transformations define the finest notion of equivalently entangled states
[
Kra10a
,
Kra10
]
.
Random quantum states are studied for instance to study generic properties of entanglement
[
Pag93
,
HLW06
,
Hay+16
,
CN15
,
DLN20
,
DM24
,
CCL26
]
, and for a distribution to be relevant in this context, it must be LU invariant. This motivates the study of LU-invariant random tensors.

Free probability (see e.g.
[
NS06
]
) allows for the study of non-commutative random variables – elements of non-commutative probability space – such as
N\times N
random matrices
M
in the limit of infinite size,
N\rightarrow\infty
. It was first introduced by Voiculescu in the 80s
[
Voi85
,
Voi86
]
in the context of operator algebras, before its application to random matrices
[
Voi91
]
. The analogues of independence, convolution, cumulants etc, become freeness, free convolution, and free cumulants, which are central to the theory. The free cumulants
\{\kappa_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
indeed contain the same information as the first order asymptotic moments
\varphi_{n}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[\Tr(M^n)/N]
,
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
, that characterize the limiting eigenvalue distribution of
M
.
One can indeed compute the former from the latter from free moment-cumulant formulae, and conversely. While for classical commutative random variables, the moment cumulants formulae involve convolutions and Moebius inversion in the lattice of partitions, it is the lattice of non-crossing partitions that plays this role for their free analogues.
As for their commutative analogues, free cumulants are additive for free random variables, and allow for efficient computations of the asymptotic spectrum of sums or products of random matrices, in particular through the use of generating functions – the resolvent or Cauchy transform and the R-transform. The latter is the free analogue of the logarithm of the Fourier transform, and it can be derived as a rescaled limit of the logarithm of the HCIZ integral. Higher order free cumulants of random matrices
[
Col+07
]
play a similar role for the infinite size asymptotics of the classical cumulants.

In the past two years, several works have undertaken the vast task of generalizing the theory of free probability for random tensors, LU-invariant
[
CGL25
,
NP25
]
, or invariant under other groups
[
KMW24a
,
BB24
,
Bon26
]
.
The starting point is the definition of a generalization of free cumulants, whose knowledge is equivalent to the correlation functions defining the theory, which as stated above are defined in terms of expectations and cumulants of trace-invariants.
From now on, motivated by the applications to quantum information theory, we restrict the discussion to LU-invariance.

For random matrices, the free-cumulants at arbitrary orders can be obtained as infinite
N
limits of some finite size precursors
[
Col+07
,
CC06
,
CC08
,
LZ25
]
, defined in a natural way from the generating functions of cumulants of the entries of the matrix. A number of the crucial properties that make free cumulants better suited to study polynomials in independent random matrices can be derived this way.
These finite
N
precursors have been generalized in a similar way in
[
CGL25
]

in full generality for pure and mixed LU-invariant random tensors. Their knowledge is equivalent to that of the classical cumulants of connected trace-invariants. In the same way as for matrices, tensorial free cumulants are then defined in this paper as the infinite size limits of these precursors, and they consequently satisfy generalized versions of the properties of usual free cumulants, such as the additivity for independent invariant random tensors. These limits are studied in the connected case (for moments) in
[
CGL25
]
for pure LU invariant random tensors for which the dominant scale in
N
of the correlations defining the distribution are the same as for the pure complex Gaussian tensor (whose coefficients are i.i.d. complex Gaussian random variables of variance
1/N^{D-1}
). Most results are derived for the moments that scale the strongest in
N
, called
first order
. Tensorial free cumulants of mixed LU-invariant random tensors are studied in the same paper under a similar scaling assumption.

In
[
NP25
]
, Nechita and Park study mixed random tensors whose moments scale like random matrices, and they adopt another starting point to define tensorial free cumulants in this context: the moment-cumulant formulae defining these quantities are directly stated “at the limit”, and then tensorial freeness is shown to hold for random tensors satisfying certain conditions, as well as a number of other properties. Nechita and Park’s study is restricted to moments, which can be seen as a restriction to connected trace-invariants.
While it is clear that this notion coincides with the pure tensorial free cumulants defined in
[
CGL25
]
for the moments that scale the strongest in
N
(first order), it remains to see whether the frameworks are compatible to higher extents.

At this stage, the examples of random tensors studied in
[
CGL25
,
NP25
]
are mostly distributions built from random vectors or random matrices, with large invariance groups and whose tensorial free cumulants trivialize the moment-cumulant formulae.
Another important point is therefore to define concrete tensor distributions whose free cumulants take non-trivial values, in the sense that they for instance allow enumerating elements in the lattice involved in the moment-cumulant formulae that satisfy.

In the present paper, we pursue the study of tensorial free cumulants, in the following directions:

•

We show in Sec.
4
that for LU-invariant random tensors satisfying the scaling assumption of
[
NP25
]
, the finite
N
precursors of the tensorial free cumulants defined in
[
CGL25
]
converge in the connected (or transitive) case to the tensorial free cumulants of Nechita and Park. We push their study further for such distributions by providing the limits in full generality, lifting the restriction to the connected case. This generalizes for such tensors the higher order free cumulants of random matrices
[
Col+07
]
. We also generalize the relation linking for matrices the free energy of the HCIZ integral and the R-transform, the generating function of free cumulants
[
GZ02
,
Col03
,
GM05
,
Col+07
]
. This clarifies why for the first order invariants, some relations that resemble the ones of
[
NP25
]
had been derived in
[
CGL23a
]
, were the asymptotics of the tensor HCIZ integral are derived under the same scaling assumption.

•

As mentioned above, until now the examples of LU-invariant random tensors studied are mostly pure or mixed random tensors with larger invariance groups. In
[
NP25
]
, a theorem is given which shows that global unitarily invariant random matrices that scale appropriately have tensorial free cumulants that either coincide with their matricial free cumulants, or vanish otherwise. In Sec.
3
, we show a similar result for finite
N
precursors, and for the more general case of a LU-invariant random tensors satisfying a coarser local unitary invariance. Our result encompasses their result asymptotically in the mixed, connected case, and extends it to the disconnected case. It asymptotically provides a universality result in the pure, global unitary invariant case. These results explain why global unitary invariant distributions do not provide examples with non-trivial tensorial free cumulants.

•

We also push the results of
[
CGL25
]
further: for pure LU-invariant random tensors that scale like the standard complex Gaussian tensor, we derive in Sec.
5
the formulae defining the cumulants of arbitrary orders, connected or not, thus also generalizing the higher order free cumulants of random matrices for such tensors (in
[
CGL25
]
, this was only done in the connected case). We also derive the inverse formulae in the connected case (in
[
CGL25
]
, this was only done at first order and few other cases). We study another scaling function, obtained for pure Gaussians with random covariances (next point), or which we derive the formulae defining the cumulants of arbitrary orders and the inverse formulae at arbitrary order, connected or not. For both these scalings, we show a result similar to that relating the free energy of the tensor HCIZ integral and the generating function of first order tensorial free cumulants, where the former is replaced by a tensor version of the BGW integral.

•

The motivation at the origin of this work was to produce concrete examples with non-trivial tensorial free cumulants. In order to do so, we study in Sec.
6
pure Gaussian random tensors with non-trivial covariance tensors, random or not. For random matrices, models of this kind are for instance relevant for the Kontsevitch model
[
Kon92
]
, dually weighted matrix models
[
KSW96
,
GM05a
]
, matrix models for causal dynamical triangulations
[
BH09
]
, and models implementing renormalization flows. Our results generalize the main ingredient -- the Gaussian with non-trivial covariance -- to the tensor case
2
2

2

See also
[
BG12
]
, which defined a tensor generalization that involves
D+1
coupled random tensors (“colored” case).
For these distributions, we derive elegant and simple formulae relating the classical and tensorial free cumulants to those of the covariance, in the deterministic and random cases. We then consider simple examples whose tensorial free cumulants take non-trivial values.

•

We finally address in Sec.
7
and Sec.
8
the question of the tensorial free cumulants for (global) products of tensors
3
3

3

Global as a matrix or vector product, in opposition to local product at the level of a single input and output, studied at first order in
[
CGL25
]
.
, which encompass the Gaussians with random covariances. We derive general formulae at finite
N
, and their asymptotics for some of the scaling assumptions mentionned above, which generalize the analogous formulae for the matrix case.

2
Notations and prerequisites

2.1
Partitions and permutations

Given
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
, we denote by
[n]
the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. The
cardinal of a finite set
S
is denoted by
\#S
. In general, we will
denote by
N
the local dimension of the tensors (the size of the index set), and by
D
the number of
inputs and outputs – sometimes called number of colors.

Partitions.

Many of the results given in the sequel are expressed using partitions
and permutations. We denote the set of partitions on
[n]
by

\mathcal{P}(n)
.
When discussing pure tensors, we will consider partitions of

[n]\cup[\bar{n}]
, where
[\bar{n}]
denotes a distinct copy of

[n]
, containing the “barred” integers
\bar{1},\ldots,\bar{n}
. We denote by
\mathcal{P}(n,\bar{n})
the set of partitions of
[n]\cup[\bar{n}]
.

Definition 2.1

(Pure partitions)
.

Let
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
. The set of pure partitions is defined by

\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)=\Bigl\{\Pi\in\mathcal{P}(n,\bar{n})\colon\forall S\in\Pi,\#S\cap[n]=\#S\cap[\bar{n}]\Bigr\}.

In general, we will use the convention that lower case
\pi
denote
usual partitions and upper case
\Pi
denote pure partitions.

We define the distinguished empty partitions

0_{n}=\Bigl\{\{i\}\colon i\in[n]\Bigr\},\quad 0_{n,\bar{n}}=\Bigl\{\{i\}\colon i\in[n]\cup[\bar{n}]\Bigr\},

and full partitions

1_{n}=\{[n]\},\quad 1_{n,\bar{n}}=\{[n]\cup[\bar{n}]\}.

These partitions are especially useful when considering the partial
order
\leq
on the lattice of partitions of a set. Given
\pi_{1}

and
\pi_{2}
two partitions of a given set, we say that
\pi_{1}
is
finer than
\pi_{2}
(or that
\pi_{2}
is coarser than
\pi_{1}
), denoted
by
\pi_{1}\leq\pi_{2}
, if for all
S_{1}\in\pi_{1}
there exists

S_{2}\in\pi_{2}
such that
S_{1}\subset S_{2}
. The join

\pi_{1}\vee\pi_{2}
of two partitions
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
is the finest
partition coarser than both
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
.

Finally, if
\pi\leq\pi^{\prime}
, then for all
S\in\pi^{\prime}
the partition

\pi|_{S}
is the partition of
S
whose blocks are the blocks of

\pi
contained in
S
.

Remark 2.2

(Join between
\mathcal{P}(n)
and
\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
)
.

In Section
7
, we shall study expressions
that involve both partitions
\pi_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(n)
and pure
partitions
\Pi_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
. We shall abuse notation and see

\pi_{1}
as a partition in
\mathcal{P}(n,\bar{n})
by adjoining
the blocks
\{\bar{1}\},\ldots,\{\bar{n}\}
. We can then consider
the partition
\pi_{1}\vee\Pi_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(n,\bar{n})
.

However, note that if
\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
and
\Pi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(n)

satisfy
\Pi^{\prime}\leq\Pi^{\prime\prime}
, then we immediately have

\Pi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
. This implies that

\pi_{1}\vee\Pi_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
.

Permutations.

We denote the set of permutations on
[n]
by
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and its
identity element by
\mathrm{id}_{n}
. For
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, we denote by

\#\sigma
the number of cycles of
\sigma
, and write
\ell(\sigma^{(i)})
for the
length of a cycle
\sigma^{(i)}
of
\sigma
. We define
\Pi(\bm{\sigma})
to be
the partition of
[n]
induced by the disjoint cycles of
\sigma
. We
extend this definition to
D
-tuples of permutations

\bm{\sigma}=(\sigma_{c})_{c=1,\ldots,D}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
by

\Pi(\bm{\sigma})=\Pi(\sigma_{1})\vee\cdots\vee\Pi(\sigma_{D}).

We define for convenience

\Pi(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\rho})=\Pi(\bm{\sigma})\vee\Pi(\bm{\rho})
for

\bm{\sigma},\bm{\rho}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
. We write
K({\bm{\sigma}})=\#\Pi({\bm{\sigma}})

and say that
{\bm{\sigma}}
is connected if
K({\bm{\sigma}})=1
.

When considering pure tensors, we may see a permutation

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
as a map
[n]\to[\bar{n}]
. We thus define the pure
partition

\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\sigma)=\Bigl\{\{i,\overline{\sigma(i)}\}\colon i\in[n]\Bigr\}.

(3)

This definition is naturally extended to
D
-tuples
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
through

\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma})=\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\sigma_{1})\vee\cdots\vee\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\sigma_{D}).

Similarly as before, we set
K_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma})=\#\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma})
, and we extend this notation to the case of several families. In particular, given an additional permutation
\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
K_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma},\eta)=\#\Bigl(\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma})\vee\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\eta)\Bigr)
.

A pure partition
\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
may be restricted to a partition in

\mathcal{P}(n)
: we write

\Pi_{[n]}=\Bigl\{S\cap[n]\colon S\in\Pi\Bigr\}.

(4)

If we fix a permutation
\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, this construction gives a bijection

\begin{cases}\mathcal{P}(n)&\to\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)\\
\pi&\mapsto\Bigl\{S\cup\eta(S)\colon S\in\pi\Bigr\}.\end{cases}

(5)

In particular, using this bijection with either
\mathrm{id}_{n}
or
\eta
we have

\Pi(\bm{\sigma}\eta^{-1})\simeq\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma}\eta^{-1},\mathrm{id}_{n})\simeq\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma},\eta).

By considering the number of blocks of these permutation, we get

K({\bm{\sigma}}\eta^{-1})=K_{\mathrm{p}}({\bm{\sigma}},\eta)
.

If
\Pi_{\mathrm{p}}({\bm{\sigma}})\leq\Pi
, it is enough to know the blocks

B
of
\Pi_{[n]}
to reconstruct the blocks
G
of
\Pi
: each
B
is
supplemented by the
\sigma_{c}(i)
, where
1\leq c\leq D
and

i\in{[n]}
.

Distances and genus.

The symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
may be seen as a metric space by
considering the graph distance associated to the Cayley graph of the
symmetric group generated by the transpositions. More explicitly,
given
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
we define
|\sigma|
to be the minimal number of
term in a factorization of
\sigma
as a product of transposition. Note
that
|\sigma|=n-\#\sigma
. We then define the distance on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}

d(\sigma,\rho)=|\sigma\rho^{-1}|\quad\text{ for }\sigma,\rho\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}.

(6)

These notations extend to the case of
D
-tuples of permutations. We
write for
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}

|\bm{\sigma}|=\sum_{c=1}^{D}|\sigma_{c}|\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\#\bm{\sigma}=\sum_{c=1}^{D}\#\sigma_{c}.

(7)

The distance
d
may be extended to a distance on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
through

d(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\rho})=\sum_{c=1}^{D}d(\sigma_{c},\rho_{c})\quad\text{ for }\sigma,\rho\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}.

(8)

If one of the tuples of permutations is of the form
{\bm{\tau}}=(\tau,\ldots,\tau)
, we use the notation
d(\bm{\sigma},\tau)
.

Given two permutations
\sigma,\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, we write
\tau\preceq\sigma
to
mean that the triangular inequality between
\mathrm{id}_{n},\tau
, and
\sigma
is an equality:

d(\mathrm{id}_{n},\tau)+d(\tau,\sigma)=d(\mathrm{id}_{n},\sigma).

(9)

Similarly, we write
\bm{\tau}\preceq\bm{\sigma}
to mean

d(\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}},\bm{\tau})+d(\bm{\tau},\bm{\sigma})=d(\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}},\bm{\sigma}).

(10)

If
\sigma,\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, the genus
g
of
(\sigma,\tau)
is defined by Euler’s relation:

\#(\sigma\tau^{-1})+\#(\sigma)+\#(\tau)-n=2K(\sigma,\tau)-2g(\sigma,\tau)\;.

(11)

Remark 2.3
.

As in
[
CGL25
, Equation (2.6)]
, we notice that
(
11
) may be rewritten as

d(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\tau})+d(\bm{\tau},\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}})-d(\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}},\bm{\sigma})=2\sum_{c=1}^{D}\Bigl(g(\sigma_{c},\tau_{c})+\#\sigma_{c}-K(\sigma_{c},\tau_{c})\Bigr).

(12)

Hence,
\bm{\tau}\preceq\bm{\sigma}
is equivalent to having for all

1\leq c\leq D
,

g(\sigma_{c},\tau_{c})=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\Pi(\tau_{c})\leq\Pi(\sigma_{c}).

Other non-negative combinatorial quantities.

Consider
\pi,\pi^{\prime},\tilde{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(n)
with
\tilde{\pi}\leq\pi

and
\tilde{\pi}\leq\pi^{\prime}
. We use the quantity (already introduced in

[
Lio22
]
):

L\bigl[\pi,\pi^{\prime};\tilde{\pi}\bigr]=\#(\tilde{\pi})-\#(\pi)-\#(\pi^{\prime})+\#(\pi\vee\pi^{\prime})\geq 0\;.

(13)

The inequality is sharp, in the sense that for every
\pi\leq\pi^{\prime}
, there exist some
\tilde{\pi}\leq\pi
such that
\pi,\pi^{\prime},\tilde{\pi}
saturate the inequality.

Similarly if

\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{D},\pi^{\prime},\tilde{\pi}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\pi}_{D}\in\mathcal{P}(n)
satisfying
for any
1\leq c\leq D
the relations
\tilde{\pi}_{c}\leq\pi_{c}
and

\tilde{\pi}_{c}\leq\pi^{\prime}
, then as remarked in
[
CGL23
]
:

L_{D}\bigl[\{\pi_{c}\},\pi^{\prime};\{\tilde{\pi}_{c}\}\bigr]=\sum_{c=1}^{D}\bigl(\#(\tilde{\pi}_{c})-\#(\pi_{c})\bigr)-\#(\pi^{\prime})+\#(\pi^{\prime}\vee\pi_{1}\vee\ldots\vee\pi_{D})\geq 0\;.

(14)

The inequality is sharp in the same sense as above.

The lower bound in (
14
) may be upgraded in particular case.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
,
\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, and
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
. We have that

L_{D}\Bigl(\{\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta)\},\Pi(\bm{\sigma});\{\Pi(\sigma_{c})\}\Bigr)\geq(D-1)(K(\bm{\sigma})-K(\bm{\sigma},\eta)).

Proof.

We are going to show that for all
1\leq c\leq D
, we have

\#\sigma_{c}-\#\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta)\geq K(\bm{\sigma})-K(\bm{\sigma},\eta).

This directly implies the result.

Let
\eta_{0}=\mathrm{id},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{l}=\eta
where
l=|\eta|
and

|\eta_{i}\eta_{i+1}^{-1}|=1
. Fix
c\in[D]
. For all
i
, if

\Pi(\eta_{i}\eta_{i+1}^{-1})\leq\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i})
then

\#\sigma_{c}-\#\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i+1})=\#\sigma_{c}-\#\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i})

and

K(\bm{\sigma})-K(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i+1})=K(\bm{\sigma})-K(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i}).

Otherwise,

\#\sigma_{c}-\#\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i+1})=\#\sigma_{c}-\#\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i})+1

and

K(\bm{\sigma})-K(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i+1})-K(\bm{\sigma})-K(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i})\in\{0,1\},

depending on whether
\Pi(\eta_{i}\eta_{i+1}^{-1})\leq\Pi(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i})
or not. We get

\begin{split}\#\sigma_{c}-\#\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta)&=\sum_{i=0}^{l-1}\Bigl(\#\sigma_{c}-\#\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i+1})-\#\sigma_{c}+\#\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i}))\\
&\geq\sum_{i=0}^{l-1}\Bigl(K(\bm{\sigma})-K(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i+1})-K(\bm{\sigma})+K(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i}))=K(\bm{\sigma})-K(\bm{\sigma},\eta)\qed\end{split}

Moebius functions.

We will constantly make use of the Moebius inversion in the lattice of
partitions
(\mathcal{P}(n),\leq)
. The Moebius function on the lattice of partitions is

\mu_{\pi}=(-1)^{\#(\pi)-1}(\#(\pi)-1)!\quad\text{ for }\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n).

(15)

It enjoys the inversion property

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)\\
\pi_{1}\leq\pi\leq\pi_{2}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\prod_{S\in\pi_{2}}\mu_{\pi|_{S}}\Bigr)=\delta_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2}}.

(16)

Remark 2.5
.

A priori the lattice
(\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n),\leq)
is a
sub-lattice of the lattice of partitions of
[n]\cup[\bar{n}]
.
Hence, its Moebius function is a priori different. In the sequel,
the functions that depend on a partition
\Pi
of
[n]\cup[\bar{n}]

will always be zero if
\Pi\notin\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
.
Furthermore, if
\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
satisfies

\Pi^{\prime}\leq\Pi
, then
\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
. It implies in
particular that if
\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)\\
\Pi^{\prime}\leq\Pi\end{subarray}}\mu_{\Pi_{[n]}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Pi\text{ partition of }[n]\cup[\bar{n}]\\
\Pi^{\prime}\leq\Pi\end{subarray}}\mu(\Pi)=\delta_{\Pi^{\prime},1_{n,\bar{n}}}.

A related function to
\mu
is the Moebius function
\mathsf{M}
of the
lattice
\mathsf{NC}(n)
of non-crossing partitions of
n
elements. We do not define this
lattice as it will not be used, but still define the function

\mathsf{M}(\sigma)=\prod_{\tilde{\sigma}\text{ cycle of }\sigma}(-1)^{\ell(\tilde{\sigma})-1}\Cat_{\ell(\tilde{\sigma})-1}\quad\text{ for }\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n},

(17)

where
\Cat_{n}=\frac{1}{n+1}\binom{2n}{n}
is the
n
-th Catalan number. This function can be extended to
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
by

\mathsf{M}(\bm{\sigma})=\prod_{c=1}^{D}\mathsf{M}(\sigma_{c})\quad\text{ for }\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}.

(18)

2.2
Trace-invariants and moments of LU-invariant random tensors

Indices of tensors

We distinguish between mixed and pure tensors. A mixed tensor
A
is a
collection of complex numbers

A=(A_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{D};j_{1},\ldots,j_{D}})
, where for

1\leq c\leq D,i_{c},j_{c}\in[N_{c}]
and
N_{1},\ldots,N_{D}\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
. A pure tensor is a collection of
complex numbers

(T,\bar{T})=(T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{D}},\bar{T}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{D}})
,
where for
1\leq c\leq D,i_{c},j_{c}\in[N_{c}]
. In most cases

\bar{T}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{D}}
is the complex conjugate of

T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{D}}
but it needs not be the case in general.

Except in Section
6
, we will assume

N_{1}=\cdots=N_{D}=N
. This can be done without loss of
generality as a
N_{1}\times\cdots\times N_{D}
tensor can always
be embedded in a
N\times\cdots\times N
tensor, up to adding
zeroes.

As a shortened notation, we will often write

A_{\bm{i};\bm{j}}=A_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{D};j_{1},\ldots,j_{D}}

and
T_{\bm{i}}=T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{D}}
with

\bm{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{D}),\bm{j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{D})\in[N]^{D}
.
A family with
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
such multi-indices will be given by a
function
\bm{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{D})\colon[n]\to[N]^{D}
. We define the composition of
a
D
-tuple of permutations
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
with such a
function by

\bm{i}\circ\bm{\sigma}=(i_{1}\circ\sigma_{1},\ldots,i_{D}\circ\sigma_{D}).

(19)

Given a family
\bm{A}=(A^{(1)},\ldots,A^{(n)})
of mixed tensors or
(\bm{T},\overline{\bm{T}})=\bigl((T^{(1)},\bar{T}^{(1)}),\ldots,(T^{(n)},\bar{T}^{(n)})\bigr)
of pure tensors, we let

\bm{A}_{\bm{i};\bm{j}}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}A^{(k)}_{\bm{i}(k);\bm{j}(k)}\quad\text{ or }\quad\bm{T}_{\bm{i}}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}T^{(k)}_{\bm{i}(k)}.

(20)

Labeled trace-invariants.

Let
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
and
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
. The trace-invariants
of a family of mixed tensors
\bm{A}=(A^{(1)},\ldots,A^{(n)})
or
of a family of pure tensors
\bm{T}=(T^{(1)},\ldots,T^{(n)})
are
defined using notation (
20
) by

\displaystyle\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}}(\bm{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{j}\colon[n]\to[N]^{D}}\bm{A}_{\bm{j}\circ\bm{\sigma};\bm{j}}=\sum_{\bm{i},\bm{j}\colon[n]\to[N]^{D}}\ \prod_{k=1}^{n}A^{(k)}_{\bm{i}(k);\bm{j}(k)}\delta_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}\circ\bm{\sigma}(k)}\;,

(21)

\displaystyle\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}}(\bm{T},\bar{\bm{T}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{j}\colon[n]\to[N]^{D}}\bm{T}_{\bm{j}\circ\bm{\sigma}}\ \overline{\bm{T}}_{\bm{j}}=\sum_{\bm{i}\colon[n]\to[N]^{D}}\ \prod_{k=1}^{n}T^{(k)}_{\bm{i}(k)}\bar{T}^{(k)}_{\bm{j}(k)}\delta_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}\circ\bm{\sigma}(k)}\;.

(22)

We write
\Tr_{\bm{\sigma}}(A)
and
\Tr_{\bm{\sigma}}(T,\bar{T})

when considering families of identical tensors. They are
generalizations of the usual trace of a matrix, recovered by taking

D=1
and
\bm{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1})
with one cycle in the mixed
trace-invariant. Going further, trace-invariants are generalizations
of the products of traces of powers of a matrix in the mixed case.
This is recovered by taking
D=1
.

In the sequel, if
S\subset[n]
, we will sometimes abuse notation and
consider
\Tr_{\bm{\sigma}_{|_{S}}}(T,\bar{T})
. By this we mean the
trace-invariant associated to the
D
-tuple of permutations

(g_{S,1}^{-1}\circ\sigma_{1}\circ f_{S},\ldots,g_{S,D}^{-1}\sigma_{D}\circ f_{S})

where
f_{S}
is the strictly increasing mapping
[\#S]\to S
and

g_{S,c}
is the strictly increasing mapping
[\#S]\to\sigma_{c}(S)
.
We use the analogous notation in the mixed case.

Graphical representation.

A trace-invariant can be represented graphically as a colored graph
with labelled vertices. Given
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
, the
graph has vertex set
[n]\cup[\bar{n}]
and each vertex is
incident to
D
colored edges as follows. For all
1\leq c\leq D

and
v\in[n]
, there is an edge of color
c
between the vertices

v
and
\sigma_{c}(v)
. Note that the graph is bipartite: the
vertices labelled by elements of
[n]
are connected to vertices
labelled by
[\bar{n}]
only.

Figure 1:
Left: a 4-colored graph. Right: a melonic 3-colored graph (the light-blue regions represent the canonical pairs).

When considering trace-invariants of a family of identical tensors,
the family of all trace-invariants

(\Tr_{\bm{\sigma}})_{\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}
contains redundant
observables. Indeed, relabeling the vertices appearing in the
graphical representation yields identical quantities. We thus define
equivalence relations corresponding to this invariance by relabeling.

We define the two equivalence relations
\sim_{\rm m}
and
\sim_{\mathrm{p}}
on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
as:

\begin{split}{\bm{\sigma}}\sim_{\rm m}{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\qquad&\Leftrightarrow\qquad\exists\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n},\quad{\bm{\sigma}}=\eta{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\eta^{-1}\;,\\
{\bm{\sigma}}\sim_{\mathrm{p}}{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\qquad&\Leftrightarrow\qquad\exists\eta,\nu\in\mathfrak{S}_{n},\quad{\bm{\sigma}}=\eta{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\nu\;.\end{split}

(23)

The sequence of numbers

(\mathds{E}\Tr_{\bm{\sigma}}(A)\colon\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}/\sim_{{\rm m}})
and

(\mathds{E}\Tr_{\bm{\sigma}}(T,\bar{T})\colon\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}/\sim_{{\rm p}})

play the role of macroscopic moments.

Given a
D
-tuple of permutation
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
, its
automorphism group for the relations
\sim_{{\rm m}}
and
\sim_{{\rm p}}

are
\Aut_{{\rm m}}(\bm{\sigma})
and
\Aut_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma})
, defined by

\Aut_{{\rm m}}(\bm{\sigma})=\{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\colon\bm{\sigma}=\eta\bm{\sigma}\eta^{-1}\}\quad\text{ and }\quad\Aut_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma})=\{(\eta,\nu)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{2}\colon\bm{\sigma}=\eta\bm{\sigma}\nu^{-1}\}.

(24)

Hence, by the orbit-stabilizer theorem, the number of
D
-tuples of
permutations
\bm{\sigma^{\prime}}
such that
\bm{\sigma}\sim_{{\rm m}}\bm{\sigma^{\prime}}
is

\frac{n!}{\#\Aut_{{\rm m}}(\bm{\sigma})}
, and the number of
\bm{\sigma^{\prime}}
such
that
\bm{\sigma}\sim_{{\rm p}}\bm{\sigma^{\prime}}
is

\frac{(n!)^{2}}{\#\Aut_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma})}
.

Melonic graphs.

A family of trace-invariants play a distinguished role in the study of
random tensors: trace-invariants related to melonic graphs (see for
instance
[
BLT17
,
FLT20
]
).
Those graphs are defined recursively as follows:

1.

either it is the only graph with vertices labelled by
1
and

\bar{1}
and
D
colored edges between them;

2.

or it is a colored bipartite graph as introduced above, which contains at least two vertices
v

and
\bar{v}
connected by
D-1
edges, and such that when
removing
v
and
\bar{v}
and connecting the two edges
connected to other vertices, we obtain a melonic graph.

This recursive definition makes it clear that there is a distinguished
pairing of vertices labelled by
[n]
with vertices labelled by

[\bar{n}]
: in case
1
, it is the pairing

1\mapsto\bar{1}
, and in case
2
it is the
pairing defined recursively as being the one sending the label of
v

to the label of
\bar{v}
. Such a pairing defines a permutation

\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
called the canonical pairing of the melonic graph.

The following quantity will play an important role
4
4

4

It is well-defined, since for every
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
and
\bm{\rho}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, there exists
\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that

K_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\rho},\eta)=K(\bm{\rho}\eta^{-1})=1.
Indeed, letting
\gamma=(1\,2\,\ldots\,n)
, the permutation
\eta=\gamma^{-1}\rho_{1}
satisfies
K(\rho_{1}\eta^{-1})=1
and thus
K_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\rho},\eta)=K(\bm{\rho}\eta^{-1})=1
.

:

\delta({\bm{\tau}})=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\\
K_{{\rm p}}({\bm{\tau}},\eta)=1\end{subarray}}d(\bm{\sigma},\eta),

(25)

Theorem 2.6
.

Consider
{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
. Then, one has that

\Omega({\bm{\sigma}})=K({\bm{\sigma}})+n(D-1)-\#\bm{\sigma}\geq 0,

(26)

with equality if and only if
{\bm{\sigma}}
is melonic and the vertices in the canonical pairs have the same label (i.e. the canonical pairing is the identity). Similarly (see e.g. Thm. 5.3 and Thm. 5.7 of
[
CGL25
]
):

\delta({\bm{\sigma}})\geq n-1+(D-1)(K_{\rm p}({\bm{\sigma}})-1)\;,

(27)

with equality if and only if
{\bm{\sigma}}
is melonic. Furthermore, if
{\bm{\sigma}}
is melonic, its canonical pairing is the unique
\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
d({\bm{\sigma}},\eta)=\delta({\bm{\sigma}})
and
K_{\rm p}({\bm{\sigma}},\eta)=K_{\rm p}({\bm{\sigma}})
.

The
{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
for which
\Omega({\bm{\sigma}})
takes a fixed positive
value can also be characterized and counted asymptotically, see

[
FLT20
]
.

2.3
Classical cumulants

The classical cumulants of a family
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
are defined using the Moebius function of the lattice of partition (
15
) through the formula

k_{p}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}(p)}\mu_{\pi}\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathds{E}\bigl[\prod_{i\in B}x_{i}\bigr].

(28)

The classical cumulants enjoy the inverse relations:

\mathds{E}[x_{1}\cdots x_{p}]=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}(p)}\prod_{B\in\pi}k_{\#B}(\{x_{i}\}_{i\in B}).

(29)

We now define the cumulants in the case of mixed and pure tensors.

Mixed version:

We denote the classical cumulants of
p
connected trace-invariants:

\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=k_{p}\bigl(\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}^{(1)}}(A),\ldots,\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}^{(p)}}(A)\bigr),

(30)

where
\bm{\sigma}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{\sigma}^{(p)}
are the connected components of
{\bm{\sigma}}
, i.e. if
\Pi(\bm{\sigma})=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{p}\}
, we have for all
1\leq i\leq p,\bm{\sigma}^{(i)}=\bm{\sigma}|_{S_{i}}
. In particular
K(\bm{\sigma})=p
. From (
28
), one has:

\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=\sum_{\pi\geq\Pi({\bm{\sigma}})}\mu_{\pi}\prod_{S\in\pi}\mathds{E}\bigl[\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}|_{S}}(A)\bigr].

(31)

Note that if
A^{\prime}
is a deterministic tensor and
A=A^{\prime}
or
A=UA^{\prime}U^{\dagger}
with
U=U_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{D}
,
U_{c}
Haar distributed, one has

\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=\delta_{\Pi({\bm{\sigma}}),1_{n}}\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}}(A^{\prime})\;.

(32)

The multiplicative extension

\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{\pi,\bm{\sigma}}[A]=\prod_{S\in\pi}\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}|_{S}}[A]

satisfies

\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{1_{n},{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]\quad\text{ and }\quad\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{\Pi({\bm{\sigma}}),{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}^{(i)}}[A].

(33)

Pure version:

In a similar fashion as in the mixed case, the classical cumulants of
a pure random tensor are defined for
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
by

\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]=k_{p}\bigl(\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}^{(1)}}(T,\bar{T}),\ldots,\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}^{(p)}}(T,\bar{T})\bigr),

(34)

where now the
{\bm{\sigma}}^{(i)}
are the
pure
connected components of

{\bm{\sigma}}
: writing
\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma})=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{p}\}
, we
set
\bm{\sigma}^{(i)}=\bm{\sigma}|_{S_{i}\cap[n]}
. The multiplicative
extension

\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\Pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]=\prod_{S\in\Pi_{[n]}}\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}|_{S}}[T,\bar{T}]

satisfies

\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{1_{n,\bar{n}},{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]=\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]\quad\text{ and }\quad\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\Pi_{{\rm p}}({\bm{\sigma}}),{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}^{(i)}}[T,\bar{T}].

(35)

2.4
Examples of LU-invariant random tensors

Two kinds of examples of LU-invariant random tensors naturally come to mind:

•

Tensors taken uniformly at random in the LU-orbit of a deterministic tensor, of the form
A=UA^{\prime}U^{\dagger}
in the mixed case, where
A^{\prime}
is a deterministic mixed tensor with
D
inputs and
U=U_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{D}
,
U_{c}
Haar distributed, and
T=UT^{\prime}
in the pure case, where
T^{\prime}
is a deterministic pure tensor with
D
inputs.

•

Unitarily invariant random matrices (GUE, Wishart…) or vectors (Gaussian, Haar distributed…) with subdivided index sets.

Standard complex Gaussians:
Fixing the number of indices
D
, the example that has been studied the most in the literature is the pure standard complex Gaussian – or pure “Ginibre” – random tensor
(T_{\mathbbm{1}},\bar{T}_{\mathbbm{1}})
, where the components of
T_{\mathbbm{1}}
are i.i.d. centered Gaussian distributed complex random variables of variance
1/N^{D-1}
:

\mathds{E}\bigl[(T_{\mathbbm{1}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{D}}(T_{\mathbbm{1}})_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{D}}\bigr]=N^{1-D}\prod_{c=1}^{D}\delta_{i_{c},j_{c}}\;.

(36)

One has in that case for any
{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
,
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
, and
\bm{i},\bm{j}\colon[n]\to[N]^{D}
(see e.g.
[
Gur14
]
):

\begin{split}\mathds{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}(T_{\mathbbm{1}})_{\bm{i}(k)}(\bar{T}_{\mathbbm{1}})_{\bm{j}(k)}\right]&=N^{n(1-D)}\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}(\eta(k))}\;,\\[4.30554pt]
\mathds{E}[\Tr_{\bm{\sigma}}(T_{\mathbbm{1}},\bar{T}_{\mathbbm{1}})]&=N^{n(1-D)}\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}N^{\sum_{c=1}^{D}\#(\sigma_{c}\eta^{-1})}\;,\\
\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T_{\mathbbm{1}},\bar{T}_{\mathbbm{1}}]&=N^{n(1-D)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\\
{K_{p}({\bm{\sigma}},\eta)=1}\end{subarray}}N^{\sum_{c=1}^{D}\#(\sigma_{c}\eta^{-1})}\;.\end{split}

(37)

Wishart tensors:
We will call Wishart tensor of parameters
(N^{D},N^{D^{\prime}})
a Wishart random matrix of parameters
(N^{D},N^{D^{\prime}})
whose indices are subdivied in
D
inputs and
D
outputs. More precisely, given a standard complex Gaussian
(T_{\mathbbm{1}},\bar{T}_{\mathbbm{1}})
as above with
D+D^{\prime}
inputs, then the coefficients of the Wishart tensor
W=(W_{\bm{i};\bm{j}})
of parameters
(N^{D},N^{D^{\prime}})
are:

W_{\bm{i};\bm{j}}=\sum_{\bm{i}^{\prime}\in[N]^{D^{\prime}}}(T_{\mathbbm{1}})_{\bm{i},\bm{i}^{\prime}}(\bar{T}_{\mathbbm{1}})_{\bm{j},\bm{i}^{\prime}},

(38)

where
\bm{i},\bm{j}\in[N]^{D}
.
Such Wishart tensors have been discussed in the case
D^{\prime}=1
in
[
CGL25
, Section 5.5]
. We get for any
{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
,
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
, and
\bm{i},\bm{j}\colon[n]\to[N]^{D}
:

\begin{split}\mathds{E}\Bigl[\prod_{k=1}^{n}W_{\bm{i}(k);\bm{j}(k)}\Bigr]&=N^{n(1-D)}\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}N^{-D^{\prime}|\eta|}\ \prod_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}(\eta(k))}\;,\\[4.30554pt]
\mathds{E}[\Tr_{\bm{\sigma}}(W)]&=N^{n(1-D)}\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}N^{\sum_{c=1}^{D}\#(\sigma_{c}\eta^{-1})-D^{\prime}|\eta|}\;,\\
\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[W]&=N^{n(1-D)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\\
{K({\bm{\sigma}},\eta)=1}\end{subarray}}N^{\sum_{c=1}^{D}\#(\sigma_{c}\eta^{-1})-D^{\prime}|\eta|}\;.\end{split}

(39)

This notion of Wishart tensor extends to different parameters. Given
D,D^{\prime}\geq 1
,
N\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
, and
\bm{N^{\prime}}=(N^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,N^{\prime}_{D^{\prime}})\in(\mathbf{N}^{*})^{D^{\prime}}
, a Wishart tensor
W
of parameters
(N^{D},\bm{N^{\prime}})
is defined as follows. Let
(T_{\mathbbm{1}},\bar{T}_{\mathbbm{1}})
be a standard Gaussian tensor of size
N\times\cdots\times N\times N^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times N^{\prime}_{D^{\prime}}
with
D+D^{\prime}
inputs, i.e. a tensor of this size with i.i.d. complex Gaussian entries of variance
N^{1-D-D^{\prime}}
. We set

W_{\bm{i};\bm{j}}=\sum_{\bm{i^{\prime}}\in[N^{\prime}_{1}]\times\cdots\times[N^{\prime}_{D^{\prime}}]}(T_{\mathbbm{1}})_{\bm{i},\bm{i^{\prime}}}(\bar{T}_{\mathbbm{1}})_{\bm{j},\bm{i^{\prime}}},\quad\text{ where }\bm{i},\bm{j}\in[N]^{D}.

For convenience, we set
r_{c^{\prime}}=\frac{N^{\prime}_{c}}{N},1\leq c^{\prime}\leq D^{\prime}
.
For any
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
,
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
, and
\bm{i},\bm{j}\colon[n]\to[N]^{D}
:

\begin{split}\mathds{E}\Bigl[\prod_{k=1}^{n}W_{\bm{i}(k);\bm{j}(k)}\Bigr]&=N^{n(1-D)}\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\Bigl(\prod_{c^{\prime}=1}^{D^{\prime}}r_{c}\Bigr)^{\#\eta}\ N^{-D^{\prime}|\eta|}\ \prod_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}(\eta(k))}\;,\\[4.30554pt]
\mathds{E}[\Tr_{\bm{\sigma}}(W)]&=N^{n(1-D)}\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\Bigl(\prod_{c^{\prime}=1}^{D^{\prime}}r_{c}\Bigr)^{\#\eta}\ N^{\sum_{c=1}^{D}\#(\sigma_{c}\eta^{-1})-D^{\prime}|\eta|}\;,\\
\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[W]&=N^{n(1-D)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\\
{K({\bm{\sigma}},\eta)=1}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\prod_{c^{\prime}=1}^{D^{\prime}}r_{c}\Bigr)^{\#\eta}\ N^{\sum_{c=1}^{D}\#(\sigma_{c}\eta^{-1})-D^{\prime}|\eta|}\;.\end{split}

(40)

2.5
Random tensors in the limit of infinite size

In Random Matrix Theory, the limiting distribution as the size
N
of
a random matrix
X_{N}
goes to infinity is defined through the limit
of the moments, appropriately renormalized

(\frac{1}{N}\mathds{E}\Tr X_{N}^{k})_{k\geq 0}
. A richer description of the

N\to\infty
limit is provided by considering the leading
asymptotics of the cumulants of the renormalized traces, i.e. the quantities

k_{n}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N}\Tr X_{N}^{k_{1}},\ldots,\frac{1}{N}\Tr X_{N}^{k_{n}}\Bigr)

(41)

for
n\geq 0
and
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\geq 0
.

Here, we study the analogous quantities for random tensors. The
analogous quantities to the one in (
41
) are the

(\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A])_{n\in\mathbf{N}^{*},\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}
in the
mixed case and

(\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T,\bar{T}])_{n\in\mathbf{N}^{*},\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}

in the pure case. Contrary to the matrix case, it is not clear in the
tensor case how one is to renormalize the classical cumulants to
obtain the sequence of numbers defining the distribution in the limit
of infinite size.

A way to choose the normalization is to follow
[
Col+07
]
and
[
CGL25
]
and define the
distribution asymptotically at first order by the data of the dominant
contributions in
N
, of the classical cumulants
\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}
that
scale the strongest in
N
. Given a sequence

(T_{N},\bar{T}_{N})_{N\geq 1}
of pure or
(A_{N})_{N\geq 1}
of mixed
random tensor, if
\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}(A_{N})
or

\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}(T_{N},\bar{T}_{N})
is non-zero for
N
big enough
for this distribution, we let:

\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A_{N}]=N^{r({\bm{\sigma}})}\Bigl(\varphi^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}(a)+o(1)\Bigr)\quad\text{ or }\quad\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T_{N},\bar{T}_{N}]=N^{r({\bm{\sigma}})}\Bigl(\varphi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}(t,\bar{t})+o(1)\Bigr)\;,

(42)

where either
\varphi^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}(a)\neq 0
in the mixed case or

\varphi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}(t,\bar{t})\neq 0
in the pure case is
independent on
N
. In the sequel, we relax the requirement that those
quantities are non-zero, and consider tensors that scale
at
most
as
N^{r(\bm{\sigma})}
, i.e. such that the scaling assumption
(
42
) is satisfied with
\varphi^{{\rm m}}
or
\varphi^{{\rm p}}
a finite
quantity that does not depend on
N
but may or may not be zero. If the coefficient
\varphi^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}(a)
or
\varphi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}(t,\bar{t})
is non-vanishing, we will say that the scaling
r
is
sharp
for this distribution. Note
that in the sequel, we will omit the index
N
keeping track of the
size of the tensors.

These quantities
\varphi_{\bm{\sigma}}
are the
asymptotic
cumulants
of the sequences of random tensors. With this
terminology, we depart from the one of
[
Col+07
]
in
which these quantities where called asymptotic moments. This notion of
asymptotic cumulants depend on the scaling
r
. We shall take as
scaling exponent natural quantities that appear when considering
tensor products of matrices in the mixed case, and Gaussian tensors in
the pure case. Note that the Gaussian scaling will have special
properties and we will be led to consider a second pure scaling, see
Section
5.1
.

One expects a sharp scaling
r
to be bounded on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
by its maximum

r_{\mathrm{M}}
(up to a global rescaling in
N
of the components of
the tensor). For a given sequence of distributions
(T_{N},\bar{T}_{N})_{N\geq 1}
or
(A_{N})_{N\geq 1}
with sharp
5
5

5

One can instead make the weaker assumption that for every
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
there is at least one first order
{\bm{\sigma}}
with non-vanishing coefficient.
scaling (
42
), we then define the asymptotic distribution in the limit of infinite size
N
at first order
by the data of the
\varphi_{\bm{\sigma}}
, for
{\bm{\sigma}}
such that

r({\bm{\sigma}})=r_{\mathrm{M}}
.
6
6

6

One could also make a choice of scaling, and define a notion of asymptotic distribution that depends on this scaling. The precise choice of definition will be unimportant for many results presented in the sequel.
Similarly, we define the distribution at
order
k\geq 1
by the data of the
\varphi_{\bm{\sigma}}
for
{\bm{\sigma}}

satisfying

r_{\mathrm{M}}-r({\bm{\sigma}})\leq k-1\;.

For classical random matrix ensembles (
D=1
),
r(\sigma)=2-\#(\sigma)
,
r_{\mathrm{M}}=1
, obtained for
\sigma
cyclic (with a single cycle).

One can go further in the definition of distributions of random tensors in the limit of infinite size. For instance, the first order distribution of a pure random tensor for which
r({\bm{\sigma}})
is the same as for a complex Gaussian takes value in a generalization of non-commutative probability spaces, see
[
CGL25
]
.

2.6
The Gaussian and Wishart scalings

The Gaussian scaling.

The moments of a standard complex Gaussian tensor are given in (
37
).
Theses moments are of order

N^{n-\delta^{\bullet}(\bm{\sigma})}
where

\delta^{\bullet}(\bm{\sigma})=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\end{subarray}}d({\bm{\sigma}},\eta).

(43)

The classical cumulants of such a Gaussian tensor are then of order

N^{n-\delta(\bm{\sigma})}
where
\delta
has been defined in (
25
):

\delta(\bm{\sigma})=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\\
K_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma},\eta)=1\end{subarray}}d(\bm{\sigma},\eta),

and are precisely

\begin{split}&\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T_{\mathbbm{1}},\bar{T}_{\mathbbm{1}}]=N^{n-\delta(\bm{\sigma})}\Bigl(\varphi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}(t_{1},\bar{t}_{1})+o(1)\Bigr),\\
&\varphi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}(t_{1},\bar{t}_{1})=\#\Bigl\{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\colon K_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma},\eta)=1,\delta(\bm{\sigma})=d(\bm{\sigma},\eta)\Bigr\}.\end{split}

(44)

From Thm.
2.6
, the first order trace-invariant correspond to purely connected melonic
{\bm{\sigma}}
, and in that case,
\varphi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}(t_{1},\bar{t}_{1})=1
.

The multiplicative extension
\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\Pi}
has its
N\to\infty

asymptotics described in terms of the following quantity, defined for

\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
:

\delta(\Pi,\bm{\sigma})=\sum_{B\in\Pi_{[n]}}\delta({\bm{\sigma}}_{\lvert_{B}})=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\\
\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma},\eta)=\Pi\end{subarray}}d(\bm{\sigma},\eta),

(45)

and the
\varphi^{{\rm p}}_{\Pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}(t_{1},\bar{t}_{1})
count the elements in the sets

\mathsf{S}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Pi,\bm{\sigma})=\Bigl\{\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\colon\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma},\eta)=\Pi,\delta(\Pi,\bm{\sigma})=d(\bm{\sigma},\eta)\Bigr\}\quad\text{ for }\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)\text{ and }\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}.

(46)

If
\Pi=1_{n,\bar{n}}
, we will use the notation
\mathsf{S}_{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{\sigma})=\mathsf{S}_{\mathcal{N}}(1_{n,\bar{n}},\bm{\sigma})
.

The Wishart scaling.

The moments of a Wishart tensor of parameters
(N^{D},N^{D^{\prime}})
are given in (
39
). For
{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
, we let
\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D^{\prime}}
be
{\bm{\sigma}}
supplemented by
D^{\prime}
copies of
\mathrm{id}_{n}
, that is:

\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D^{\prime}}=({\bm{\sigma}},\mathrm{\bf id})=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{D},\mathrm{id}_{n},\ldots,\mathrm{id}_{n})\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D+D^{\prime}}\;.

With these notations, from (
39
), the moments of
W
are of order
N^{n-\delta^{\bullet}(\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D^{\prime}})}
, and since
K_{\rm p}(\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D^{\prime}},\eta)=K({\bm{\sigma}},\eta)
, the classical cumulants satisfy:

\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[W]=N^{n-\delta(\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D^{\prime}})}\varphi^{{\rm p}}_{\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D^{\prime}}}(t_{1},\bar{t}_{1})\;.

(47)

Corollary 2.7

(of Thm.
2.6
)
.

Consider
D^{\prime}\geq 1
and
{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
with
K({\bm{\sigma}})
fixed to
K
, one has

n-\delta(\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D^{\prime}})\leq 1-(D+D^{\prime}-1)(K-1)\;,

with equality if and only if
\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D^{\prime}}
is melonic. For
D^{\prime}\geq 2
, this occurs if and only if
{\bm{\sigma}}
is melonic and the canonical pairing is the identity.

Proof.

The first statements result from Thm.
2.6
together with the fact that
K_{\rm p}(\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D^{\prime}})=K({\bm{\sigma}})
. If
D^{\prime}\geq 2
,
\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D^{\prime}}
is melonic if and only if
{\bm{\sigma}}
is melonic and the canonical pairing is the identity. ∎

The first order therefore again consists in melonic
{\bm{\sigma}}
, which are purely connected for
D^{\prime}\geq 1
, but are allowed to be connected but not purely connected for
D^{\prime}=1
, see Sec. 5.5 of
[
CGL25
]
.

For
D^{\prime}=D
, we may write the scaling
n-\delta(\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D^{\prime}})
in a way which does not involve a minimum.

Proposition 2.8
.

Consider
{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
,
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
. Then:

n-\delta(\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D})=2D\bigl(1-K({\bm{\sigma}})\bigr)+\#{\bm{\sigma}}-n(D-1).

The proof relies on the following lemma:

Lemma 2.9
.

Consider
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
,
\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
, such that
K({\bm{\sigma}},\eta)=1
. Then:

d(\bm{\sigma},\eta)+d(\eta,\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}})-d(\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}},\bm{\sigma})\geq 2D\big(K({\bm{\sigma}})-1\bigr)\;,

and for every
{\bm{\sigma}}
, there exists
\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that equality holds and
K({\bm{\sigma}},\eta)=1
.

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

Consider
\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
K({\bm{\sigma}},\eta)=1
. From Lemma
2.9
:

\begin{split}n-&d(\hat{\bm{\sigma}}_{D},\eta)\\
&=n-d(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}})-2D\bigl(K({\bm{\sigma}})-1\bigr)-\Bigl(d(\bm{\sigma},\eta)+d(\eta,\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}})-d(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}})-2D\bigl(K({\bm{\sigma}})-1\bigr)\Bigr)\\
&\leq\#\bm{\sigma}-2D\bigl(K({\bm{\sigma}})-1\bigr)-n(D-1).\end{split}

Still from Lemma
2.9
, there exists a
\eta\in\mathfrak{S}
such that
K({\bm{\sigma}},\eta)=1
and the quantity between parenthesis vanishes. This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.9
.

For
{\bm{\tau}}=(\eta,\ldots,\eta)
, (
12
) reads:

d(\bm{\sigma},\eta)+d(\eta,\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}})-d(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}})=2\sum_{c=1}^{D}g(\sigma_{c},\eta)+\sum_{c=1}^{D}\bigl[\#(\sigma_{c})-K(\sigma_{c},\eta)\bigr],

and using (
14
):

L_{D}\Bigl[\{\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta)\},\Pi(\bm{\sigma});\{\Pi(\sigma_{c})\}\Bigr]=\sum_{c=1}^{D}\Bigl(\#(\sigma_{c})-\#(\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta)\Bigr)-K({\bm{\sigma}})+1\geq 0\;.

we write:

d(\bm{\sigma},\eta)+d(\eta,\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}})-d(\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}},\bm{\sigma})=2\big(K({\bm{\sigma}})-1\bigr)+2\sum_{c=1}^{D}g(\sigma_{c},\eta)+2L_{D}\bigl[\{\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta)\},\Pi({\bm{\sigma}});\{\Pi(\sigma_{c})\}\bigr]\;,

which is lower bounded by
2D(K(\bm{\sigma})-1)
by Lemma

2.4
. It reaches this value if we choose
\eta
as
follows. We construct it inductively. If
K(\bm{\sigma})=1
, then we can
choose
\eta=\mathrm{id}_{n}
. Otherwise, we are going to construct
permutations
\eta_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{n},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{K(\bm{\sigma})-1}

such that for all
0\leq i<K(\bm{\sigma})
,
|\eta_{i}|=i
,

K(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i})=K(\bm{\sigma})-i
,
g(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i})=0
for all

1\leq c\leq D
, and

L_{D}\Bigl[\{\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i})\},\Pi(\bm{\sigma});\{\Pi(\sigma_{c})\}\Bigr]=(D-1)i.

These assumptions are clearly satisfies for
\eta_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{n}
.
Assume we have constructed
\eta_{i}
with
i<K(\bm{\sigma})-1
. Hence,

K(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i})\geq 2
. Let
p,q\in[n]
such that
p
and
q

belongs to different blocks of
\Pi(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i})
. Set

\eta_{i+1}=\eta(p\,q)
. We get that
|\eta_{i+1}|=|\eta_{i}|+1
and
K(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i+1})=K(\bm{\sigma},\eta_{i})-1
, that for all
1\leq c\leq D
,

\begin{split}g(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i+1})&=K(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i+1})+\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(n-\#\sigma_{c}-\#\eta_{i+1}-\#(\sigma_{c}\eta_{i+1}^{-1})\Bigr)\\
&=K(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i})-1+\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(n-\#\sigma_{c}+1-\#\eta_{i+1}-\#(\sigma_{c}\eta_{i+1}^{-1})+1\Bigr)=g(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i})=0,\end{split}

we used that since
p
and
q
are in different blocks of
K(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i})
, we have that
\#\sigma_{c}\eta_{i+1}^{-1}=\#\sigma_{c}\eta_{i}^{-1}-1
. It remains to show the property about
L_{D}
: we have
\#\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i+1})=\#\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i})-1
so that

L_{D}\Bigl[\{\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i+1})\},\Pi(\bm{\sigma});\{\Pi(\sigma_{c})\}\Bigr]=L_{D}\Bigl[\{\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta_{i})\},\Pi(\bm{\sigma});\{\Pi(\sigma_{c})\}\Bigr]+D-1=(D-1)(i+1).

Finally,

L_{D}\Bigl[\{\Pi(\sigma_{c},\eta)\},\Pi(\bm{\sigma});\{\Pi(\sigma_{c})\}\Bigr]=(D-1)(K(\bm{\sigma})-1),

and Lemma
2.4
gives that this is the smallest possible value.

Finally, with this choice of
\eta
, we have

d(\bm{\sigma},\eta)+d(\eta,\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}})-d(\bm{\mathrm{id}_{n}},\bm{\sigma})=2\big(K({\bm{\sigma}})-1\bigr)+2(D-1)(K(\bm{\sigma})-1)=2D(K(\bm{\sigma})-1).\qed

2.7
Weingarten functions

The Weingarten calculus allows the computation of moments of entries
of unitary matrices distributed according to the Haar measure. The
fundamental result is the following.

Theorem 2.10

(Weingarten formula,
[
Wei78
,
Sam80
,
Col03
]
)
.

Let
n,N\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
and
i,j,i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\colon[n]\to[N]
. We have

\int_{\mathbb{U}(N)}U_{i(1)j(1)}\cdots U_{i(n)j(n)}\overline{U_{i^{\prime}(1)j^{\prime}(1)}\cdots U_{i^{\prime}(n)j^{\prime}(n)}}\ \differential U=\sum_{\rho,\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\delta_{i,i^{\prime}\circ\rho}\delta_{j,j^{\prime}\circ\sigma}{\rm W}_{N}(\sigma\rho^{-1}),

where
\mathbb{U}(N)
is the unitary group of
N\times N
unitary
matrices,
\differential U
denotes the Haar measure on
\mathbb{U}(N)
, and

{\rm W}_{N}
is the
Weingarten function
defined for

N\geq n
by

{\rm W}_{N}(\sigma)=\int_{\mathbb{U}(N)}U_{1,1}\cdots U_{n,n}\overline{U_{1,\sigma(1)}\cdots U_{n,\sigma(n)}}\ \differential U\quad\text{ for }\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}.

In the sequel, we shall use the notation

{\rm W}_{N}(\bm{\nu})=\prod_{c=1}^{N}{\rm W}_{N}(\nu_{c})\quad\text{ for }\bm{\nu}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}.

(48)

A remarkable identity is that for any
n\leq N
and any
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
:

\sum_{\nu\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}N^{\#(\nu\tau^{-1})}{\rm W}_{N}(\nu)=\delta_{\tau,\mathrm{id}}\;.

(49)

It will be useful to apply to the Weingarten function the same Moebius
transformation used to go from the moments to the classical cumulants.
Given
{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
and
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)
with

\Pi(\bm{\nu})\leq\pi
, we define the Weingarten cumulant functions
(
[
CGL23
]
and
[
Lio22
]
) as:

{\rm W}^{{\rm m}}_{{\rm C},N}[\pi,{\bm{\nu}}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(n)\\
\pi\leq\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mu_{\pi^{\prime}}\prod_{B\in\pi^{\prime}}\prod_{c=1}^{D}{\rm W}_{N}(\nu_{c}{{}_{|_{B}}}).

(50)

Given
\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
with
\Pi(\bm{\nu})\leq\Pi_{[n]}
, we define the
pure analogue:

{\rm W}^{{\rm p}}_{{\rm C},N}[\Pi,{\bm{\nu}}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)\\
\Pi\leq\Pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mu_{\Pi^{\prime}}\prod_{B\in\Pi^{\prime}_{[n]}}\prod_{c=1}^{D}{\rm W}_{N}(\nu_{c}{{}_{|_{B}}}).

(51)

Remark 2.11
.

We notice that if
\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
satisfies

\Pi(\bm{\nu})\leq\Pi_{[n]}
, we immediately have

{\rm W}^{{\rm p}}_{{\rm C},N}[\Pi,\bm{\nu}]={\rm W}^{{\rm m}}_{{\rm C},N}[\Pi_{[n]},\bm{\nu}].

Conversely, given
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)
with
\Pi(\bm{\nu})\leq\pi
,
we can make use of the bijection mentioned in Section

2.1
. We let
\pi_{{\rm p}}\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
which is
such that both
(\pi_{{\rm p}})_{[n]}
and

(\pi_{{\rm p}})_{[\bar{n}]}
coincide with
\pi
and similarly for
any
\pi^{\prime}\geq\pi
. As pure partitions,

\pi^{\prime}_{{\rm p}}\geq\pi_{{\rm p}}\geq\Pi_{{\rm p}}({\bm{\nu}})\vee\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\mathrm{id})
.
Then:

{\rm W}^{{\rm m}}_{{\rm C},N}[\pi,{\bm{\nu}}]={\rm W}^{{\rm p}}_{{\rm C},N}[\pi_{{\rm p}},{\bm{\nu}}]\;.

(52)

In order to compute the asymptotics of the cumulant Weingarten
function, we introduce the sets of monotone walks.

Definition 2.12

(Monotone walk)
.

Let
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
,
r\in\mathbf{N}
, and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. The set of monotone walks
to
\sigma
with
r
steps is

\mathsf{w}^{r}(\sigma)=\biggl\{(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{r})\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{r}\colon\exists(a_{i},b_{i}),\forall i,\tau_{i}=(a_{i}\,b_{i});a_{i}<b_{i};b_{1}\leq\cdots\leq b_{r};\tau_{r}\cdots\tau_{1}=\sigma\biggr\}.

The planar monotone Hurwitz number is then

\gamma(\sigma)=\#\Bigl\{\bm{\tau}\in\mathsf{w}^{n+\#\sigma-2}\colon\Pi(\sigma)\vee\Pi(\tau_{1})\vee\cdots\vee\Pi(\tau_{r})=1_{n}\Bigr\}.

Remark 2.13
.

A monotone walk can be seen as a subsets of ramified coverings of
the sphere
S^{\prime}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}
with labelled sheets where one point has
ramification profile given by
\sigma
and
r
points have simple
ramification profile specified by the
\tau_{i}
’s. Hence,
Riemann-Hurwitz formula gives

\chi(S^{\prime})=n-r+\#\sigma.

(53)

This implies that whenever

\Pi(\sigma)\vee\Pi(\tau_{1})\vee\cdots\vee\Pi(\tau_{r})=1_{n}
,

n-r+\#\sigma\leq 2,

with equality if and only if
S^{\prime}
has the topology of a sphere. This
explains why
\gamma(\sigma)
is called the
planar
monotone Hurwitz number.

The asymptotics of the cumulant Weingarten functions are then computed
in Theorem
2.14
. This Theorem
was shown with different notation in
[
CGL23
, Theorem
3.3]
, and in the mixed case for case
D=1
in

[
CGL23a
]
. See also
[
Lio22
, Section
2.5]
.

Theorem 2.14
.

For any
{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
,
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)
that
satisfies
\Pi(\bm{\nu})\leq\pi
, and
\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)
that
satisfies
\Pi(\bm{\nu})\leq\Pi_{[n]}
, the cumulant Weingarten
function admits the following asymptotics:

\begin{split}{\rm W}^{{\rm m}}_{{\rm C},N}[\pi,{\bm{\nu}}]&=N^{\#\bm{\nu}-2(\#\pi-1)-2nD}\Bigl(\Gamma[\pi,{\bm{\nu}}]+\order{N^{-1}}\Bigr)\\
{\rm W}^{{\rm p}}_{{\rm C},N}[\Pi,{\bm{\nu}}]&=N^{\#\bm{\nu}-2(\#\Pi-1)-2nD}\Bigl(\Gamma[\Pi_{[n]},{\bm{\nu}}]+\order{N^{-1}}\Bigr)\\
\end{split}

(54)

where

\Gamma[\pi,{\bm{\nu}}]=(-1)^{|\bm{\nu}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{D}\in\mathcal{P}(n)\\
\forall c,\Pi(\nu_{c})\leq\pi_{c}\\[1.50694pt]
{\pi\vee\pi_{1}\vee\ldots\vee\pi_{D}=1_{n}}\\[1.50694pt]
{L_{D}[\{\pi_{c}\},\;\pi\;;\;\{\Pi(\nu_{c})\}]=0}\end{subarray}}\prod_{c=1}^{D}\prod_{B_{c}\in\pi_{c}}\gamma({\nu_{c}}_{\lvert_{B_{c}}})\;,

(55)

where
L_{D}
has been defined in (
14
) and
\gamma
in Definition
2.12
.

Remark 2.15

(Connected case)
.

If
\pi=1_{n}
, we get that

\Gamma[1_{n},\bm{\nu}]=\prod_{c=1}^{D}\Bigl((-1)^{|\nu_{c}|}\prod_{S\in\Pi(\nu_{c})}\gamma(\nu_{c}|_{S})\Bigr)=\prod_{c=1}^{D}\Bigl((-1)^{|\nu_{c}|}\prod_{S\in\Pi(\nu_{c})}\Cat_{\#S-1}\Bigr)=\mathsf{M}(\bm{\nu}).

Since almost identical results have been proved in
[
CGL23
,
CGL23a
]
, we defer the proof to App.
A
.

2.8
Tensorial free cumulants from finite size precursors

The quantities we define as free cumulants are obtained by taking
limits of natural objects obtained for finite
N
from the tensorial
generalization of the Harish-Chandra–Itzykson–Zuber and
Brézin–Gross–Witten integrals.

2.8.1
Moments and cumulants of the tensor HCIZ and BGW integrals

The tensor Harish-Chandra–Itzykson–Zuber (HCIZ) integral was defined in
[
CGL23
,
CGL23a
]
as

\mathcal{I}_{D,N}[A;B]=\int[dU]\ \mathrm{e}^{\Tr(B^{\trans} U A U^\dagger)}\;,

(56)

where
B^{\mathsf{T}}
is the transpose of
B
, and
U=U_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{D}
,
U_{c}
is Haar distributed, and
[dU]=dU_{1}\cdots dU_{D}
.

As shown in
[
CGL23
]
, the moments of

\mathcal{I}_{D,N}[A;B]
satisfy:

\int[dU]\left[\Tr(B^{\trans} U A U^\dagger)\right]^{n}=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\Tr_{{\bm{\tau}}}[B]\;\mathcal{G}_{\bm{\tau}}[A]\;,\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{G}_{\bm{\tau}}[A]=\sum_{{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\Tr_{{\bm{\nu}}}(A){\rm W}_{N}(\bm{\nu}\bm{\tau}^{-1})\;.

(57)

These last relations can be inverted for
n\leq N
using (
49
), so that
for
n\leq N
the
\mathcal{G}_{{\bm{\tau}}}
determine the trace-invariants:

\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}}(A)=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}N^{nD-d({\bm{\sigma}},{\bm{\tau}})}\mathcal{G}_{{\bm{\tau}}}[A]\;.

(58)

In the pure case, letting
J\cdot T=\sum_{\bm{i}}J_{\bm{i}}T_{\bm{i}}
, one rather defines:

\mathcal{J}_{D,N}[T,\bar{T};J,\bar{J}]=\int[dU]\ \mathrm{e}^{J\cdot UT+\bar{J}\cdot\bar{U}\bar{T}}\;,

(59)

which can be seen as a particular case of tensor Brezin-Gross-Witten (BGW) integral
[
GW80
,
BG80
]

\mathcal{J}_{D,N}[B]=\int[dU]\ \mathrm{e}^{\Tr(B^\dagger U + B U^\dagger)}\;,

(60)

for
\bar{B}=J\otimes T
. A consequence of
[
CGL25
, Theorem
4.10]
is that the moments of

\mathcal{J}_{D,N}[J,\bar{J};T,\bar{T}]
expand as:

\int[dU]\ \left(J\cdot T\right)^{n}\left(\bar{J}\cdot\bar{T}\right)^{n}=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\Tr_{{\bm{\tau}}}[J,\bar{J}]\;\mathcal{G}_{\bm{\tau}}[T,\bar{T}]\;.

(61)

The inverse relation (
58
) is similar in the pure case.

In
[
LZ25
]
, for the matrix case (
D=1
), Lacroix and Zuber call the
\mathcal{G}_{\tau}[A]
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbf{N}^{\star}
the “finite

N
precursors of free cumulants” for matrices: it is another basis
for LU-invariant polynomials, and in the large
N
limit their
rescaled limits converge to products of (first-order) free cumulants
for the disjoint cycles of
\sigma
. The quantity
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
corresponds to the "
\mathbb{U}
-cumulants" of Capitaine and Casalis
[
CC06
,
CC08
]
, in the case of families of random matrices.

For
{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
and
\pi\geq\Pi({\bm{\sigma}})
, we let

{\mathcal{G}}_{\pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=\prod_{B\in\pi}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\tau}}_{|_{B}}}[A]
,
and we define the classical cumulants associated to the
\mathcal{G}_{\bm{\sigma}}
:

\mathcal{L}^{\rm m}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=\sum_{\pi\geq\Pi({\bm{\sigma}})}\mu_{\pi}{\mathcal{G}}_{\pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\Tr_{\bm{\tau}}(A)\ {\rm W}^{{\rm m}}_{{\rm C},N}\bigl[\Pi({\bm{\sigma}},{\bm{\tau}}),{\bm{\sigma}}{\bm{\tau}}^{-1}\bigr]\;.

(62)

It only differs from the
\mathcal{G}
in the disconnected case:

\textrm{If }\ \Pi({\bm{\sigma}})=1_{n},\qquad\mathcal{L}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A]={\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]\;.

(63)

They are the coefficients of the expansion of the free-energy of
\mathcal{I}_{D,N}[A;B]
on the trace-invariants of
B

[
CGL23
]
:

\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial z^{n}}\log\mathcal{I}_{D,N}[A;zB]\Bigr\lvert_{z=0}=\sum_{{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}}(B)\;\mathcal{L}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A]\;.

(64)

If for instance
A
is the
N^{D}\times N^{D}
identity matrix
\mathbbm{1}
:

\Tr_{\bm{\sigma}}(\mathbbm{1})=N^{\#\bm{\sigma}}\;,\qquad\mathcal{G}_{\bm{\sigma}}[\mathbbm{1}]=\delta_{{\bm{\sigma}},\bm{\mathrm{id}}}\;,\qquad\mathcal{L}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[\mathbbm{1}]=\delta_{{\bm{\sigma}},\bm{\mathrm{id}}}\ \delta_{\Pi({\bm{\sigma}}),1_{n}}=\delta_{n,1}\;.

(65)

We also define the pure analogue:

\mathcal{L}^{{\rm p}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]=\sum_{\Pi\geq\Pi_{{\rm p}}({\bm{\sigma}})}\mu_{\Pi}\ {\mathcal{G}}_{\Pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\Tr_{\bm{\tau}}(T,\bar{T})\ {\rm W}^{{\rm p}}_{{\rm C},N}\bigl[\Pi_{{\rm p}}({\bm{\sigma}},{\bm{\tau}}),{\bm{\sigma}}{\bm{\tau}}^{-1}\bigr]\;.

(66)

In particular, in the purely connected case:

\textrm{If }\ \Pi_{\mathrm{p}}({\bm{\sigma}})=1_{n,\bar{n}},\qquad\mathcal{L}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T,\bar{T}]={\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]\;,

(67)

but the
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}
usually differ otherwise.

The computations of
[
CGL25
, Section A.2]
in the
particular case where
T=UT^{\prime}
with
U=U_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{D}
,
U_{c}
a tensor
product of Haar distributed unitary matrices (in analogy to

[
CGL23
]
), show that the quantities

\mathcal{L}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}
appear as coefficients of the expansion of the
free-energy of
\mathcal{J}_{D,N}[T,\bar{T};J,\bar{J}]
on the
trace-invariants of
J,\bar{J}
:

\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial z^{n}}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial\bar{z}^{n}}\log\mathcal{J}_{D,N}[T,\bar{T};zJ,\bar{z}\bar{J}]\Bigr\lvert_{z=\bar{z}=0}=\sum_{{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}}(J,\bar{J})\;\mathcal{L}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T,\bar{T}]\;.

(68)

2.8.2
Mixed finite size precursors

For random tensors, we recall the construction of

[
CGL25
]
. For
A
random,
{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
, and

\pi\geq\Pi({\bm{\sigma}})
, we let

\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}[A]=\mathds{E}\bigl[\mathcal{G}_{{\bm{\tau}}}(A)\bigr],\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\pi,{\bm{\tau}}}[A]=\prod_{B\in\pi}\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\tau}}_{|_{B}}}[A]\;,

(69)

and define the finite
N
precursors of the free cumulants in the mixed
case as the classical cumulants associated to the quantities

\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\bm{\tau}}[A]
:

\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)\\
\Pi({\bm{\sigma}})\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\mu_{\pi}\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]\;,

(70)

which expresses
\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A]
in terms of the

\mathds{E}\bigl[\Tr_{{\bm{\tau}}_{|_{G}}}(A)\bigr]
for
G\in\pi
. This can be
rewritten in terms of the classical cumulants as

\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A]=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)\\
\Pi(\bm{\tau})\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{\pi,{\bm{\tau}}}[A]\ {\rm W}^{{\rm m}}_{{\rm C},N}\bigl[\pi\vee\Pi({\bm{\sigma}}),{\bm{\sigma}}{\bm{\tau}}^{-1}\bigr]\;,

(71)

where
{\rm W}^{{\rm m}}_{{\rm C},N}
has been defined in
(
50
).

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
and
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
. The inverse
relation of (
70
) in terms of the moments are:

\mathds{E}\left[\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}}(A)\right]=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}N^{nD-d({\bm{\sigma}},{\bm{\tau}})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)\\
\Pi(\bm{\tau})\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\ \mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\pi,{\bm{\tau}}}[A]\;,

(72)

in terms of the quantities
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
:

\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)\\
\Pi(\bm{\sigma})\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]\;,

(73)

in terms of the classical cumulants:

\Phi^{{\rm m}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}N^{nD-d({\bm{\sigma}},{\bm{\tau}})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\pi\geq\Pi({\bm{\tau}})}\\
{\Pi({\bm{\sigma}})\vee\pi=1_{n}}\end{subarray}}\ \mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\pi,{\bm{\tau}}}[A]\;.

(74)

This result is an application of Moebius inversion. Arguments of this
sort are commonplace when discussing free cumulants, see for instance

[
NS06
]
. For the convenience of the reader, we give
a short argument.

Proof.

We start by proving (
73
). By summing over partitions, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)\\
\Pi(\bm{\sigma})\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)\\
\Pi(\bm{\sigma})\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(n)\\
\Pi({\bm{\sigma}})\leq\pi^{\prime}\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\prod_{S\in\pi}\mu_{\pi^{\prime}|_{S}}\Bigr)\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\pi^{\prime},{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]\;,

We exchange the sums on
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
, and use Moebius inversion (
16
),
this immediately gives (
73
).

To get (
72
), we use
(
58
) and (
73
):

\mathds{E}\Bigl[\Tr_{\bm{\sigma}}[A]\Bigr]=\sum_{\bm{\tau}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}N^{nD-d(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\tau})}\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\bm{\tau}}[A]=\sum_{\bm{\tau}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}N^{nD-d(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\tau})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)\\
\Pi(\bm{\sigma})\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}[A].

Finally, one get (
74
) from
from (
31
) and
(
72
), and the fact that

\sum_{\tilde{\pi}\leq\pi}\mu_{\tilde{\pi}}=\delta_{\pi,1_{n}}
.
∎

In the connected case, one recovers the expectations of the quantities derived from the tensor HCIZ integral:

\textrm{If }\ \Pi({\bm{\sigma}})=1_{n},\qquad\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A]=\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=\mathds{E}\bigl[\mathcal{L}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A]\bigr]\;,

(75)

but these relations do not usually hold anymore for non-connected
{\bm{\sigma}}
.
If
A^{\prime}
is a deterministic tensor and
A=A^{\prime}
or
A=UA^{\prime}U^{\dagger}
with
U=U_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{D}
,
U_{c}
Haar distributed, the relation between
\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}
extends to arbitrary
{\bm{\sigma}}
, connected or not:

\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[A]=\mathcal{L}^{\rm m}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[A^{\prime}]\;.

(76)

2.8.3
Pure finite size precursors

In the pure case, recalling that
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
is
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\Pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]=\prod_{B\in\Pi_{[n]}}\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}_{|_{B}}}[T,\bar{T}]
, we have:

\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T,\bar{T}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)\\
\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma})\leq\Pi\end{subarray}}\mu_{\Pi}\,\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\Pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]\;.

(77)

If
{\bm{\sigma}}
is purely connected, we get the simpler relation

\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T,\bar{T}]=\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]
. The relation
(
77
) can be rewritten in terms of the
classical cumulants as

\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T,\bar{T}]=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)\\
\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\tau})\leq\Pi\end{subarray}}\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{\Pi,{\bm{\tau}}}[T,\bar{T}]\ {\rm W}^{{\rm p}}_{{\rm C},N}\bigl[\Pi\vee\Pi_{{\rm p}}({\bm{\sigma}}),{\bm{\sigma}}{\bm{\tau}}^{-1}\bigr]\;,

(78)

where
{\rm W}^{{\rm p}}_{{\rm C},N}
has been defined in (
51
).

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
and
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
. The inverse
relation of (
70
) in terms of the moments are:

\mathds{E}\left[\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}}(T,\bar{T})\right]=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}^{D}_{n}}N^{nD-d({\bm{\sigma}},{\bm{\tau}})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)}\\
\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\tau})\leq\Pi\end{subarray}}\ \mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{\Pi,{\bm{\tau}}}[T,\bar{T}]\;,

(79)

in terms of the quantities
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
:

\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T,\bar{T}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)\\
\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\sigma})\leq\Pi\end{subarray}}\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{\Pi,{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]\;,

(80)

in terms of the classical cumulants:

\Phi^{{\rm p}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}N^{nD-d({\bm{\sigma}},{\bm{\tau}})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Pi\in\mathcal{P}_{{\rm p}}(n)\\
\Pi_{{\rm p}}(\bm{\tau})\leq\Pi\\
{\Pi_{{\rm p}}({\bm{\sigma}})\vee\Pi=1_{n,\bar{n}}}\end{subarray}}\ \mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{\Pi,{\bm{\tau}}}[T,\bar{T}]\;.

(81)

We omit the proof of Lemma
2.17
as once one relies on Remark
2.5
, the proof is almost identical to the one of Lemma
2.16
.

In the purely connected case, one recovers the expectations of the quantities derived from the tensor BGW integral:

\textrm{If }\ \Pi_{\mathrm{p}}({\bm{\sigma}})=1_{n,\bar{n}},\qquad\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T,\bar{T}]=\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]=\mathds{E}\bigl[\mathcal{L}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T,\bar{T}]\bigr]\;.

(82)

These relations do not usually hold anymore if
{\bm{\sigma}}
is not purely connected. If
T^{\prime}
is a deterministic tensor and
T=T^{\prime}
or
T=UT^{\prime}
with
U=U_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_{D}
,
U_{c}
Haar distributed, one has for arbitrary
{\bm{\sigma}}
:

\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[T,\bar{T}]=\mathcal{L}^{{\rm p}}_{{\bm{\sigma}}}[T^{\prime},\bar{T}^{\prime}]\;.

(83)

2.8.4
Tensorial free cumulants

We define the free cumulant for mixed and pure tensors as the properly
rescaled limits of the finite-
N
precursors to the free cumulants,

\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}
in the mixed case and
\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}
in the pure case. The limits

\kappa^{{\rm m}}
and
\kappa^{{\rm p}}
enjoy several properties that make them natural
quantities to study the limiting distibution. Firstly, they are
related to the
\varphi^{{\rm m}}
and the
\varphi^{{\rm p}}
discussed in Section

2.5
by inversion relations. The precise
form these relations take will be discussed below, but we note that
they are obtained as a
N\to\infty
limit of the relations given in
Lemmata
2.16
and
2.17
. Secondly, they
satisfy additivity properties. These properties are stated in
[
CGL25
, Proposition 4.9 and Equation
(4.25)]
.

Proposition 2.18

(Additivity at finite
N
)
.

Let
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
be two independent LU-invariant random mixed tensors, and

(T_{1},\bar{T}_{1})
and
(T_{2},\bar{T}_{2})
be two independent
LU-invariant random pure tensors. Assume that all the moments of these tensors
exist. Then, for all
n\in\mathbf{N}^{*}
and
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
, there exist
N_{n}
such that for every
N\geq N_{n}
:
we have in the mixed case

\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A_{1}+A_{2}]=\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A_{1}]+\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A_{2}],

and in the pure case

\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T_{1}+T_{2},\bar{T}_{1}+\bar{T}_{2}]=\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T_{1},\bar{T}_{1}]+\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[T_{2},\bar{T}_{2}].

Proposition
2.18
implies additivity properties of
the free cumulants
\kappa^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\sigma}}
and
\kappa^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\sigma}}
at all
orders, whether
\bm{\sigma}
is connected or not.

2.9
Microscopic versus macroscopic

In this subsection, we show that it is equivalent to define the distribution of a LU-invariant random tensor by the data of its macroscopic moments – the expectations of trace-invariants – or by the data of the microscopic moments – the joint moments of the tensor components.

We recall the following (the expression is the same in the pure case replacing
A
by
(T,\bar{T}
):

\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}[A]=\sum_{{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\mathds{E}[\Tr_{{\bm{\nu}}}(A)]\,\prod_{c=1}^{D}W^{(N)}(\nu_{c}\tau_{c}^{-1})\;.

(84)

Proposition 2.19
.

If
A
(respectively
(T,\bar{T})
) is a mixed (respectively pure) LU-invariant random tensor with
D
inputs, then the joint moments of the tensor components can be computed from the expectations of trace-invariants as:

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}A_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}(k)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}\circ\bm{\tau}(k)}\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}[A]\;,

(85)

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}T_{\bm{i}(k)}\bar{T}_{\bm{j}(k)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}\circ\bm{\tau}(k)}\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}[T,\bar{T}]\;.

(86)

For every
D
and if
n\leq N
, the coefficients
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}[A]
and
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}[T,\bar{T}]
in these expressions are respectively unique up to
\sim_{\rm m}
and
\sim_{\mathrm{p}}
, see (
23
).

Similarly, for the classical cumulants of the tensor components, one has the same formulae with
\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{\bm{\tau}}[A]
and
\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{\bm{\tau}}[T,\bar{T}]
on the right hand side:

\displaystyle k_{n}\left(\left\{A_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}(k)}\right\}_{1\leq k\leq n}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}\circ\bm{\tau}(k)}\mathcal{K}^{{\rm m}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}[A]\;,

(87)

\displaystyle k_{n}\left(\left\{T_{\bm{i}(k)}\bar{T}_{\bm{j}(k)}\right\}_{1\leq k\leq n}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}\circ\bm{\tau}(k)}\mathcal{K}^{{\rm p}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}[T,\bar{T}]\;.

(88)

Proof.

The formulae in the statement are obtained using the LU-invariance
and the Weingarten integration formula, Theorem

2.10
. Alternatively, one can use the
generating function of microscopic moments, the LU-invariance of

A
, as well as (
57
):

\begin{split}\mathds{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}A_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}(k)}\right]&=\Bigl(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial B_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}(k)}}\Bigr)\log\mathds{E}\left[e^{\Tr(B^{\trans}A)}\right]\Bigr\lvert_{B=0}\\
&=\Bigl(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial B_{\bm{i}(k),\bm{j}(k)}}\Bigr)\sum_{{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}}\Tr_{{\bm{\sigma}}}(B)\;\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\bm{\sigma}}[A]\;.\end{split}

Knowing (
85
), we derive the
expectation of (
58
), namely:
